Discussion Name:
Has Religion Been a Good Thing for Humanity?

1. Thesis:
Religion has been a good thing for humankind.

  1.1. Con:
  Religious institutions are often exploitative.

    1.1.1. Pro:
    Religious institutions and beliefs have often been associated with abuse.

      1.1.1.1. Pro:
      Religious institutions have helped conceal and enable abusers.

        1.1.1.1.1. Pro:
        Officials in the Church of England have often silenced and ignored abuse allegations [1].

        1.1.1.1.2. Pro:
        The Catholic church molestation scandals [2] show that, until pressed by secular ones, religious authorities are content to hide their crimes.

          1.1.1.1.2.1. Con:
          The Congregation of the Servants of the Paraclete [3] was founded in the 1940's by Fr. Gerald Fitzgerald to expose and help prevent child sexual abuse by the clergy.

            1.1.1.1.2.1.1. Con:
            Fitzgerald started to assist priests dealing with substance abuse but soon began receiving priests who had sexually abused minors. He did not bring them to justice and was against secular treatments [4] like psychiatry and psychology.

            1.1.1.1.2.1.2. Con:
            Fitzgerald recommended some priests to be laicized but, in general, most bishops chose to ignore [5] his recommendations and kept employing priests with sexual abuse history.

            1.1.1.1.2.1.3. Con:
            The center was closed [6] in the 1990s as the result of lawsuits over priests who had molested children while staying at the center or after being treated at the facility.

            1.1.1.1.2.1.4. Con:
            Abusive priests from all over the US where brought to the Monastery run by the Servants of the Paracletes since 1947. Very often, those priests were released to work with non-white and poor communities [7] and very often sexual abuses continued. In that sense, the church disposed of abusive priest in predominately white and mid-western communities to bring them to poorer and brown communities, without ever truly eradicating sexual abuse.

              1.1.1.1.2.1.4.1. Pro:
              Fr. Edward Graff (included in the Pennsylvania grand jury report [8]) was moved from Allentown where the report alleges “raped scores of children” to the Monastery. Eventually, in 1980, he was released and worked in Texas and New Mexico until being arrested for sexually abusing [9] a minor. In the investigation, it was discovered he would employ and potentially abuse young and poor Hispanic youth. He died in prison before trial.

        1.1.1.1.3. Pro:
        Bishops have been allowing priests to abuse children [10] for decades in Australian [11] Christian Churches.

        1.1.1.1.4. Pro:
        There are laws that favour churches over morality, such as those that allow priests to not report confessed crimes.

          1.1.1.1.4.1. Pro:
          In Australia, churches have said if the law changes and priests legally have to report child abuse to police, especially in a confession [12], that the priests [13] would break the law [14]. So, even when the law forces priests to report [15] it, they might not.

          1.1.1.1.4.2. Pro:
          In the UK, Catholic Priests are still encouraged not to break the seal of confession [16], even in cases where child sex abuse or other serious violent crimes have been committed.

      1.1.1.2. Pro:
      Numerous religious schools have been widely accused of perpetrating child abuse [17].

        1.1.1.2.1. Pro:
        In Canada the United Church committed numerous abuses [18] against indigenous children.

        1.1.1.2.2. Pro:
        Christian-run schools prohibited [19] native children from speaking their languages and continuing their cultural practices.

      1.1.1.3. Pro:
      The sexual abuse associated with religion creates profound trauma [20] in victims.

        1.1.1.3.1. Pro:
        Child molestation and abuse by priests in America cost more than $3.3 billion [21] in settlements over the past 15 years.

      1.1.1.4. Con:
      John Jay researchers reported that about 4 % of the 110,000 priests studied had been accused of sexual misconduct involving children. There is no consensus about the sexual abuse among the general American male population, but estimates are 1 in 10 men or close to 5% and thus the rate of abuse by Catholic priests is not higher [22] than the national estimates.

        1.1.1.4.1. Con:
        A report estimated about 7% of clergy [23] had abused children between about 1950 and 2000.

        1.1.1.4.2. Con:
        The study was "authorized and is being paid for by the U.S. Conference of Catholic Bishops [24]".

        1.1.1.4.3. Con:
        The 4% doesn't account for the accomplices who covered it up. Convicted layers of authority have reached at least two levels [25] above the initial perpetrator.

      1.1.1.5. Pro:
      Religion has often failed to condemn [26] domestic abuse, and some of its tenets [27] have been used to justify [28] it.

        1.1.1.5.1. Pro:
        Many pastors [29] have said and many continue to say that they would never advise a women to leave an abusive husband, and many advise forgiveness and enduring.

          1.1.1.5.1.1. Con:
          Recently church leaders such as the Pope and bishops have openly condemned this behavior and with more women pastors entering the ranks the responses are finally catching up to secular standards.

        1.1.1.5.2. Pro:
        In Islam, men have authority over women, and some interpretations of the Qur'an allow domestic violence [30] against women.

        1.1.1.5.3. Con:
        The Bible [31] teaches husbands to "love their wives as they love their own bodies." Men who do this are good husbands who strive to never be abusive.

          1.1.1.5.3.1. Con:
          Self-flagellation [32], an admitedly rare Christian practise, nevertheless sets a bad precedent were such a practitioner to love his wife as he loves his own body.

          1.1.1.5.3.2. Con:
          The passage the parent is from, Ephesians 5:25-29 [33] puts it in context: It emphasizes the body over the person, the wife as a physical idol, the husband's toy to wash to show his righteousness. A degree of sanity returns in the afterthought, Ephesians 5:33, "However, each one of you also must love his wife as he loves himself", but still the wife is not treated as her own person but as an extension of the man, and what if the man hates and/or abuses himself?

        1.1.1.5.4. Pro:
        The Bible teaches [34] followers to beat their children, a practice commonly considered to be abuse by today's standards.

        1.1.1.5.5. Pro:
        Research [35] points that domestic abuse is higher in regions across the globe "where norms related to male authority over female behavior are more common".

    1.1.2. Pro:
    Many religious institutions are corrupt and exploit the vulnerable for financial gain [36].

      1.1.2.1. Con:
      Religion has motivated numerous selfless acts such as volunteering and charity.

        1.1.2.1.1. Con:
        Religions hypocritically teach compassion, equality, and moderation; many religious institutions remain incredibly wealthy [37] while much of the world lives in poverty.

          1.1.2.1.1.1. Pro:
          Jesus is clearly quoted [38] as saying that it is easier for a camel to fit through the eye of a needle [39] than for a wealthy person to enter heaven.

          1.1.2.1.1.2. Con:
          Plenty [40] of clergy, and other religious followers, do take vows of poverty.

            1.1.2.1.1.2.1. Con:
            While some clergy and religious officials disavow wealth, they still collect money on behalf of the church or in accordance with religious doctrine.

          1.1.2.1.1.3. Pro:
          There is reason [41] to believe that Scientology may have been founded to provide financial gain for L.R. Hubbard.

          1.1.2.1.1.4. Pro:
          The idea of the prosperity gospel [42] has been spreading in the US in recent times and the main idea behind it is that donating money to religious institutions will make you healthier and wealthier in the long run.

            1.1.2.1.1.4.1. Pro:
            As a result of the prosperity gospel many megachurch [43] leaders [44] have their own private jets and exorbitant amounts of money while their congregations remain impoverished or at average income.

            1.1.2.1.1.4.2. Pro:
            Many televangelists have exploited their poor followers [45], promising them financial blessings if they make donations.

          1.1.2.1.1.5. Pro:
          Mother Teresa [46] was considered a saint for the poor, yet has been accused of using her substantial funds [47] to support her own community and the Vatican, while allowing her patients to suffer unnecessarily.

            1.1.2.1.1.5.1. Pro:
            -> See discussion #16522: Mother Teresa should not have been canonized.

            1.1.2.1.1.5.2. Con:
            This could be said about most, if not all, medical practitioners, who regularly have more resources and access to better care than their patients.

          1.1.2.1.1.6. Con:
          This is an argument demonstrating religious hypocrisy stems from religious institutions and not religion itself.

            1.1.2.1.1.6.1. Con:
            With the manpower and resources that religion has, hunger and poverty should have already been abolished, but religious selfless acts tend to favor only the ones within their communities.

              1.1.2.1.1.6.1.1. Con:
              Abolishing hunger and poverty would only work if one perfect person was in control of all wealth and influence. This is utopia.

              1.1.2.1.1.6.1.2. Pro:
              Some estimations [48] say eliminating world hunger would be 30 billion a year [49], since the Catholic church has a net worth [50] of 30 billion [51] and the Mormon Church, for example, 40, it seems feasible that they could completely or almost end hunger.

                1.1.2.1.1.6.1.2.1. Con:
                This does not take into account the operational costs of the churches (maintaining buildings, bills, salaries, etc.) that could not be used for charity.

                1.1.2.1.1.6.1.2.2. Con:
                If it would cost 30 billion a year to end world hunger than two organizations who are worth 70 billion together would only be able to end it or start the process of ending it for 2 years and a third before running out of money.

                1.1.2.1.1.6.1.2.3. Pro:
                The catholic church is divided into Dioceses and Parishes. In some places the diocese is an incorporated entity and in some the enclosed parishes are separate entities as well. [52] As such the wealth of the entire organization is virtually impossible to calculate and the 30 billion figure is only representative of the Vatican and does not include all the dioceses which have their own finances.

            1.1.2.1.1.6.2. Con:
            It is impossible to separate religions from the institutions [53] that represent them.

        1.1.2.1.2. Pro:
        Before modern states could provide welfare, religion was the only source of education, healthcare or economic relief.

          1.1.2.1.2.1. Pro:
          The Catholic Church was the only institution in Europe during several hundred years that actively contributed to free education for the masses.

            1.1.2.1.2.1.1. Pro:
            The Church also mandated in 1179 free education [54] for the poor, so every cathedral had a master to teach boys too poor to pay the regular fee, planting the seed of modern universal and public education.

          1.1.2.1.2.2. Pro:
          Judaic religious tradition has throughout history emphasised education [55] which has been an advantage to its adherents at places and times where illiteracy was the norm, for example in Europe in the early middle ages [56].

            1.1.2.1.2.2.1. Con:
            Judaic education in reading was historically reserved for boys.

          1.1.2.1.2.3. Con:
          The family was also a source of education, healthcare, and economic relief.

          1.1.2.1.2.4. Con:
          If the benefits that religion provides can be now outsourced by other means (state, welfare, legal system, etc.) that are not paired with the negatives of religion, it may have been good and yet it no longer is.

          1.1.2.1.2.5. Pro:
          Basic hygienic rules [57] have been written as religious texts [58] to enforce them as higher truths. This reduced illness and death.

            1.1.2.1.2.5.1. Pro:
            Jewish hygene practices may partially explain why Jews were remarkably unaffected [59] by the Black Death, a plague that affected Europe in the mid-14th century.

            1.1.2.1.2.5.2. Con:
            Some religious practices, although seemingly hygienic (burying feces in the ground and having a bath after a trip to the latrine), were actually a way to spread parasites [60] and increase disease.

            1.1.2.1.2.5.3. Con:
            Laws of "ritual purity" even though they correspond to "modern ideas of physical cleanliness", in many cases have "little to do with hygiene [61]".

            1.1.2.1.2.5.4. Con:
            Although some religions helped to disseminate hygiene rules, this was not always true. The Roman practice of public baths was seen as 'sinful' by early Christians [62], who abhorred nudity and went on a crusade against what was seen as a Pagan luxury. The condemnation was not unanimous, but it was prevailing.

          1.1.2.1.2.6. Pro:
          Hospitals were founded, financed, and staffed by religions. An old French term [63] for hospital is hôtel-Dieu, "hostel of God."

        1.1.2.1.3. Con:
        Charity is sometimes used to perpetuate inequality.

          1.1.2.1.3.1. Con:
          Charity allows society to bring the disenfranchised back into the social contract.

          1.1.2.1.3.2. Pro:
          Charity, for example, often endorses inequality instead of fixing its roots.

            1.1.2.1.3.2.1. Con:
            Research [64] suggests that income inequality may trigger human empathy and thus it is positively associated with the probability to actively participate in charitable organizations.

            1.1.2.1.3.2.2. Pro:
            Charity [65] validates injustices and it is not an effective response to economic inequality.

            1.1.2.1.3.2.3. Pro:
            Charity is subjective and creates categories: those deserving of it and those who do not.

            1.1.2.1.3.2.4. Pro:
            Some charities give to those in need but do very little [66] in fighting for policy changes like increased minimum wages or other welfare policies.

          1.1.2.1.3.3. Pro:
          Charity serves to make the charitable person feel good with him or herself, while making the recipient of charity feel dependent and inferior. It might be better if, instead of charity, the true causes of inequality were analyzed.

            1.1.2.1.3.3.1. Pro:
            With charity one establishes a relationship of dependence with one end of the relationship deemed morally, economically and socially superior, while the other is seen as inferior, as an unproductive and degraded burden.

          1.1.2.1.3.4. Pro:
          Some charity to third world countries holds them back, fostering aid dependenc [67]y and eliminating self-reliance.

          1.1.2.1.3.5. Pro:
          There is little motivation for religions to act against poverty as lasting change would lower the contributions they receive.

            1.1.2.1.3.5.1. Pro:
            Studies (e.g. This on researchgate.net [68]) have shown that the poor give a greater percentage of their budget to charity than the rich. This means that charity is not necessarily a good thing because it's possibly receiving more from the needy than it can justify from its spending on the needy (maybe different needy), particularly when a certain proportion of contributions goes to a religion's expenses, savings, and investments in buildings, paintings, sculptures etc.

            1.1.2.1.3.5.2. Pro:
            Religious policy against contraception produces large families and thus poverty and thus higher contributions to the religion.

        1.1.2.1.4. Pro:
        Ramadan [69], which is the act of fasting, helps believers understand poverty and thus be inclined to be charitable, help and share.

          1.1.2.1.4.1. Con:
          Conversely, the experience of fasting may convince believers that poverty is not such a bad thing, leading them to abandon any efforts to fix the issue of poverty

            1.1.2.1.4.1.1. Pro:
            Some people believe the old saying, "What doesn't kill us makes us stronger" and thus may believe they are helping by exploiting people.

          1.1.2.1.4.2. Con:
          People who live in freedom can fast, abandon their possessions (temporary or permanently) and experience all types of circumstances without religion.

            1.1.2.1.4.2.1. Pro:
            For example the Erasmus [70] program which allows students to "live" other cultures in order to embrace peace and democratic coexistence.

          1.1.2.1.4.3. Con:
          Fasting does not show how poverty is, since when people live in poverty they don't know for certain that there will be food at sunset. Starvation until sunset is different from indefinite starvation. Those who fast are not being poor by starving until sunset.

          1.1.2.1.4.4. Con:
          Fasting during Ramadan is very easy: those fasting can eat as much as they want from sunset to sunrise. Generally, people don't need to eat at night, as they had food during the day. Ramadan just reverses the timings from day to night.

        1.1.2.1.5. Con:
        Discounting donations [71] going to the religion itself, non-secular people tend to donate more to charity.

          1.1.2.1.5.1. Con:
          Donations to religious organizations should be counted since a portion of them end up going to charitable causes.

            1.1.2.1.5.1.1. Pro:
            Out of the estimated $170 billion [72] annual spending of the Catholic Church in the US, about 57% (roughly $98 billion) is devoted to healthcare networks, and 28% goes to colleges. Day-to-day operations account only for 6%, and 2.7% is devoted to national charitable activities (p.12 [73]); the remaining 6.3% is used for other purposes.

              1.1.2.1.5.1.1.1. Con:
              2.7% devoted to charitable activities barely counts as charitable. It's certainly less than most people contributing to the plate would consider apt, especially with 57% going to healthcare networks considering that a large proportion of this 57% would not be necessary if not for the huge amount of price gouging in the US, which should not have to be made up by the Catholic Church.

              1.1.2.1.5.1.1.2. Con:
              A US study [74] found that 85% of Roman Catholic dioceses surveyed discovered embezzlement [75] of church money in the last five years.

            1.1.2.1.5.1.2. Con:
            It is impossible to calculate how much of the donations to any given church or religious group goes to charity as an aggregate statistic.

          1.1.2.1.5.2. Pro:
          Secular [76] individuals contribute to charity at similar rates as religious individuals, indicating that religion is not a key motivator for generosity.

            1.1.2.1.5.2.1. Pro:
            Religion is not the motivation, it only appeals to existing human values. These values would lead to selfless acts without religion.

              1.1.2.1.5.2.1.1. Pro:
              Many moral philosophies which don't depend on God, such as Kantian moral philosophy [77], also promote volunteerism and beneficence, without allowing for the possibility of abuse and misinterpretation by one group of people. Such philosophies could found virtuous acts instead of religion.

              1.1.2.1.5.2.1.2. Pro:
              Religion had the monopoly of charity for several years and for sure this affected the way we volunteer now. But we are human and in our nature we are supposed to live in "herds", so we'd find a way to help each other even without religion. It's a matter of life or death.

              1.1.2.1.5.2.1.3. Con:
              Religion can act as motivation. For example, many religious followers perform good deeds because they think it will grant them access to a positive afterlife.

                1.1.2.1.5.2.1.3.1. Con:
                The concept of a positive afterlife is part of a good confidence trick, where heaven is the carrot and hell is the stick.

              1.1.2.1.5.2.1.4. Pro:
              Many argued advantages of religion in the form of moral parables or other appeals to empathy [78], in reality, precede [79] religion, as they are merely a component of mirror neurons. Humanity in no way needs religion to achieve morality or selflessness.

                1.1.2.1.5.2.1.4.1. Pro:
                The predisposition toward caring has been observed [80] in a wide variety of animals. Neurophilosophy and neurosciences [81] in general tend to show that morality as we know it, might be a survival tool given by evolution processes. The better a species get as using this tool, the better it will perform in an evolutionary context.

              1.1.2.1.5.2.1.5. Pro:
              Experts suggest that those who are more charitable, are not so for their religious or humanists beliefs, but based on the social connections [82] and ties they have with charities.

                1.1.2.1.5.2.1.5.1. Pro:
                In that sense, if "secular [83] organizations could replicate the sort of tight, interlocking friendship networks found within religious organizations" it is likely they can achieve a "comparable level of charitable giving".

            1.1.2.1.5.2.2. Pro:
            To assert that religion is the driving force of charity, then it should be true that in the absence of religion, the same people would not have found other motivating factors for their selfless actions. Religious belief and selfless actions may share a common cause, such as a desire for meaning or connection to others. Or causation may run from selflessness to religious belief.

              1.1.2.1.5.2.2.1. Con:
              Religion can be a driving force for charity while not being the sole driving force for charity. Religion may be "the medicine one needs" for one person to facilitate a charitable heart, science for another.

            1.1.2.1.5.2.3. Con:
            Studies [84] have shown that on average, most religious states in the US give more to charity than less religious ones.

              1.1.2.1.5.2.3.1. Con:
              The data provided does not investigate the impact of religion on charity and it is therefore misinforming.

              1.1.2.1.5.2.3.2. Con:
              -> See 1.1.2.1.5.2.1.5.

              1.1.2.1.5.2.3.3. Con:
              Research [85] has shown that children who receive a religious upbringing (Christian or Muslim) are less generous and altruistic than their atheist contemporaries.

            1.1.2.1.5.2.4. Pro:
            Many atheists and agnostics volunteer and take part in charity, which suggests that these acts have little or nothing to do with religion.

              1.1.2.1.5.2.4.1. Con:
              These atheists overwhelming live and were raised in countries and cultures which at some point derived their moral and ethical principles from religious sources. All of the most major religions spiritually incentivise charity.

                1.1.2.1.5.2.4.1.1. Con:
                The Humanist ideals, which diverged [86] from religion a long time ago, also support a wide range of secular charities [87]. It is disingenuous to associate this voluntary work with a religious influence.

              1.1.2.1.5.2.4.2. Pro:
              A recent Gallup poll [88] suggests that atheists/agnostics donate roughly the same amount of money to charity as religious people.

                1.1.2.1.5.2.4.2.1. Con:
                The Gallup poll [89] has a small sample size, around 1,050 people, and about 15% [90] self-identify as without religion. This means that from their random sampling they have roughly 157 agnostic/atheist individuals, which amounts to little evidence to support the parent.

              1.1.2.1.5.2.4.3. Pro:
              -> See 1.1.2.1.5.2.3.3.

        1.1.2.1.6. Pro:
        Religious organizations make it easier for people to come together and help their communities.

          1.1.2.1.6.1. Pro:
          Religious organizations build schools and endorse universities [91], orphanages, and other services for community benefits.

          1.1.2.1.6.2. Con:
          This is a function of community itself, not religion. There are many non-religious communities that facilitate helping others.

            1.1.2.1.6.2.1. Pro:
            Due to past charitable acts, people believe that the church is the place to go to do charitable acts. However, other charities exist, and are more easily available than going to a church.

          1.1.2.1.6.3. Pro:
          Organised religion created successful and well-known platforms for humanitarian aid, such as the Order of Malta [92].

          1.1.2.1.6.4. Pro:
          In places "with little to no social supports, the local church [93] may provide for people’s basic needs through free childcare programs, food pantries, and clothing drives".

            1.1.2.1.6.4.1. Con:
            Since religion may condition help to those who belief and are part of a group, social services would be better provided by the community itself through the state.

          1.1.2.1.6.5. Con:
          Having people go to sectarian centers to foster community also fosters division between those sects that would not be there had they all just gone to a universal community center.

            1.1.2.1.6.5.1. Con:
            Many religious communities engage [94] with those of different faiths.

        1.1.2.1.7. Con:
        Many religious-based charity movements tie the spread of propaganda [95] and conservative values [96] to the offered aid, so their charity and volunteering are not necessarily selfless.

          1.1.2.1.7.1. Con:
          Religious-based charities consider the religious teachings they spread a good thing; in their opinion it is not morally reprehensible to give aid with religious propaganda attached.

            1.1.2.1.7.1.1. Con:
            Having the opinion that an action you take is not wrong does not change whether it is actually wrong or not.

            1.1.2.1.7.1.2. Con:
            When the teachings have practical consequences that are negative for those helped (death, STDs, etc.) that propaganda is indeed harmful.

              1.1.2.1.7.1.2.1. Pro:
              Missionary hospitals [97] in Africa, for example, even though they heal the sick, also "kill by denying women their rights to abortion and to reproductive health services".

              1.1.2.1.7.1.2.2. Pro:
              The organization CHASTE (Churches Against Sex Trafficking in Europe) which helps women rescued from sex trafficking does so "outside government scrutiny [98]", making women almost prisoners and with measures like confiscating their phones, confinement, high control and forced pray.

            1.1.2.1.7.1.3. Con:
            Carrying out forced [99] baptism on infants, forced conversion on death beds or forced administration of sacrament to patients in missionary hospitals reduces human freedom and dignity and it is thus unethical.

            1.1.2.1.7.1.4. Pro:
            Religious workers or missionaries attempt to help people materialistically (providing shelter, food or healthcare) but also spiritually (converting them to religion and thus saving their souls).

          1.1.2.1.7.2. Pro:
          This wastes time and resources that would be better spent on giving actual aid.

          1.1.2.1.7.3. Pro:
          Aid is given to those without power, and therefore they may be forced to accept propaganda in order to accept the aid that goes with it.

            1.1.2.1.7.3.1. Pro:
            Leo Igwe, head of the Nigerian Humanist Movement, expains that religious aid in Africa is a "Trojan horse": Humanitarian projects "are evangelising weapons [100] which missionaries use to get Africans to embrace Christianity" and "Missionary schools are religious indoctrination centres".

              1.1.2.1.7.3.1.1. Pro:
              -> See 1.1.2.1.7.1.2.1.

            1.1.2.1.7.3.2. Pro:
            Spreading ideas in order to persuade or convert people when they are in a situation of vulnerability and, thus there is an asymmetric power dynamic, is morally wrong. People should be persuaded and be part of a religion willingly not coerced or out of desperation.

          1.1.2.1.7.4. Pro:
          Mother Teresa has been accused of using her charities to forcefully convert [101] the poor and vulnerable.

            1.1.2.1.7.4.1. Pro:
            Mother Teresa instructed [102] nuns to baptise those who were about to die even when they had no knowledge of the Church's teachings.

            1.1.2.1.7.4.2. Pro:
            Mother Teresa conspired to “brainwash” [103] Hindus into Christianity and helped fuel a violent military insurgency in India’s northeast according to Yogi Adityanath, an outspoken MP in the current BJP government.

            1.1.2.1.7.4.3. Con:
            Mother Teresa did not see "active" evangelism as a necessary component of her ministry to the poor, writing "We never try to convert those who receive [aid from Missionaries of Charity] to Christianity but in our work we bear witness to the love of God’s presence..." (Life in the Spirit: Reflections, Meditations and Prayers, pp. 81-82 [104]).

            1.1.2.1.7.4.4. Con:
            Mother Teresa encouraged [105] dying Hindus to pray to their own gods, revealing her universalist beliefs. It is unlikely that such a person would also coerce conversions.

          1.1.2.1.7.5. Pro:
          In some occasions, religion is only charitable or helps those who believe.

            1.1.2.1.7.5.1. Pro:
            Some religions only organize charity for those they believe deserve [106] it. For example, numerous indictments of those like the Salvation Army [107] refusing to help people because they were LGBT, or even because they were Atheist.

            1.1.2.1.7.5.2. Pro:
            Caritas, one of the largest's world humanitarian organizations, asked members in Vietnam to combine charity with evangelization [108].

            1.1.2.1.7.5.3. Pro:

This "us versus them" mentality is the biggest con of religious groups.

        1.1.2.1.8. Pro:

            1.1.2.1.8.1.1. Con:

Some relocated permanently to the area. All charitable organizations and churches can do this. [115] Many others did, too.

For example, Points of Light [118], Travelers Aid [119], the Red Cross [120], among others, or even students [121] who sacrificed Spring Break to help in such disasters.

            1.1.2.1.8.2.1. Con:

      1.2.1.1. Con:

        1.2.1.1.1. Con:

          1.2.1.1.2.1. Pro:

            1.2.1.1.2.2.1. Con:
            From a democratic perspective, having absolute power and being infallible and thus not able to be challenged is a negative aspect on itself, regardless if the person exercising the absolute power have good or bad intentions. Additionally, what can be good for some, can be bad for others.

            1.2.1.1.2.2.2. Con:

            1.2.6.4.4.2.1. Pro:
            Religions may hamper positive self-change. Religions teach to last and endure while going through hardships, even when the solution is to change yourself.

            1.2.6.4.4.2.2. Pro:
            Religion has restricted people in enjoying the fullness of life because of "traditions" that doesn't make any sense

            1.2.6.4.4.2.3. Pro:
            -> See 1.2.5.2.3.1.7.

            1.2.6.4.4.2.4. Pro:
            The fear of hell can lead to intense feelings of guilt for insignificant events or actions that are deemed acceptable by society writ large.

              1.2.6.4.4.2.4.1. Con:
              In every [339] Christian denomination in America, belief in a Heaven is stronger than a belief in a Hell.

                1.2.6.4.4.2.4.1.1. Con:
                While 72% of Americans say to believe in Heaven, 58% of adults also believe in Hell, which makes for a large number of people who can fear that sins in this life will condemn them in the afterlife.

                1.2.6.4.4.2.4.1.2. Con:
                US Christians [340], compared with non-Christians and the unaffiliated, are more likely to believe in both afterlife destinations.

                1.2.6.4.4.2.4.1.3. Con:
                The Apocalypsis Book [341] has spread a sense of imminent destruction of our planet, blaming a god's decision; instead of raising a meditation about the consequences of our decisions and lifestyle.

              1.2.6.4.4.2.4.2. Pro:
              Surveys [342] show [343] large numbers of people believe in hell. A Gallup international poll [344] suggests that 49% of people believe in hell.

              1.2.6.4.4.2.4.3. Pro:
              A religion is bad for the psycho social wellness of its followers if it is based on lies. To tell people that God delights in having people jabbed with pitchforks makes them think He is cruel. Very deleterious to the mental well being of those who live in fear of it.

              1.2.6.4.4.2.4.4. Pro:
              Many religious people in the LGBT community feel intense fear, guilt, and cognitive dissonance due to their own nature being seen as sinful.

          1.2.6.4.4.3. Pro:
          Fear of hell can drive people to do despicable things.

            1.2.6.4.4.3.1. Con:
            Fear of hell in religion is generally closely linked to a moral dogma that compels people to be the religion's version of good.

              1.2.6.4.4.3.1.1. Con:
              Hell is part of the moral dogma since it is the punishment given to those who do not follow the dogma. In the case where the dogma instructs a despicable thing then the fear or hell will compel them to do it and there is no dogma that prevents it since the dogma is causing it.

            1.2.6.4.4.3.2. Con:
            Religion teaches that hell is a penalty for the despicable things people do, not an incentive for driving people to do despicable things.

              1.2.6.4.4.3.2.1. Con:
              No, religion teaches that hell is a penalty for not doing things according to its tenets, and those tenets are not only absolute but are written and interpreted by fallible humans who thus pass their failings on to people who inevitably end up doing despicable things.

            1.2.6.4.4.3.3. Pro:
            Some religious groups mandate female genital mutilation [345] with the fear of being barred from heaven if the procedure is not done. The same is true for Jews and circumcision [346].

          1.2.6.4.4.4. Con:
          No person has the ability to consign another person to hell; therefore, expectations of divine retribution are the responsibility of those who receive the message, not the proselytizer.

      1.2.6.5. Pro:
      Religion is often imposed on those young enough to be impressionable, leaving them stuck with it at least until they're old enough to question it, which could be a decade or so, or maybe the rest of their life if they're not that smart.

        1.2.6.5.1. Pro:
        A religion that really wants to promote free thinking, unlike Catholicism, would wait until their potential believers are old enough to commit to the faith and understand everything that it entails.

      1.2.6.6. Pro:
      Religion has a history of monopolizing education and knowledge, which limits the perspectives to which people are exposed and makes them easier to control.

        1.2.6.6.1. Pro:
        The Catholic Church used the Inquisition [347] to prosecute anyone who espoused views deemed as heretic (for example, some scientific ideas) convicting people to different sentences, including death by burning.

          1.2.6.6.1.1. Con:
          The Spanish Inquisition [348] is an example of a monarchy defying religious doctrine for malicious self interests; this was an abuse of political, not religious, authority.

            1.2.6.6.1.1.1. Con:
            The Spanish Inquisition [349] operated under royal Christian authority, yet many of the sentences pronounced during Inquisition trials were made within the judicial system [350] of the Roman Catholic Church.

          1.2.6.6.1.2. Pro:
          Cecco d'Ascoli was the first university scholar burned [351] by the Inquisition.

          1.2.6.6.1.3. Pro:
          Italian Giordano Bruno [352] was tortured and killed by the church of Rome for having introduced the concept of infinity of the universe, the multiplicity of the worlds, and the motion of the Earth respect to the sun.

          1.2.6.6.1.4. Pro:
          Jan Hus [353] was burned at the stake for heresy against the doctrines of the Catholic Church on July, 6th 1415; Czechs still commemorate this tragedy with a national holiday.

        1.2.6.6.2. Pro:
        The Vatican Apostolic Archive [354] holds secret documents, which are not available to anyone but high ranked priests or the Pope.

        1.2.6.6.3. Pro:
        -> See 1.2.5.2.5.1.4.

        1.2.6.6.4. Con:

    1.2.7. Pro:
    Religion cultivates political power which can then be used to exert control.

      1.2.7.1. Con:

      1.2.7.2. Pro:

        1.2.7.2.1. Pro:

      1.2.7.3. Pro:

        1.2.7.3.1. Pro:

        1.2.7.3.2. Pro:
        Personal freedoms are often restricted by laws such as Kashrut or Sharia.

          1.2.7.3.2.1. Con:
          Personal freedom may not be the highest value. For example, conservatives usually prioritize personal responsibility over freedom.

        1.2.7.3.3. Con:
        Without resorting to political order, a Religion cannot force its citizens to adopt a set or rules. It may only suggest a moral code by which to live.

          1.2.7.3.3.1. Con:
          The way a political order forces citizens to follow rules is by threat of consequences. Religions have done the same thing with their own threats and consequences. Religions have used excommunication, inquisitions, pogroms, and burning at the steak to enforce their rules and values.

          1.2.7.3.3.2. Con:
          In the past and even nowadays, religious authorities and religious groups have often pressured legislators to sanction laws according to their beliefs

            1.2.7.3.3.2.1. Con:
            Everyone pressures their legislators to sanctions laws according to their opinions and beliefs, this is neither a bad thing nor unique to religion. It's simply how (most) western governments work.

              1.2.7.3.3.2.1.1. Con:
              While lobbying in general is common in most places secularism and separation of church and state [356] is also valued in many modern societies.

              1.2.7.3.3.2.1.2. Con:
              In the USA churches can lose their tax exemption [357] if they endorse political candidates because it is recognized that religion is not something that should be influencing government the same way as everything else.

        1.2.7.3.4. Pro:
        Religious laws are being created even nowadays, the most numerous of which are probably the anti-LGBT ones in the US [358].

          1.2.7.3.4.1. Pro:
          Religion can be used as a protective barrier to further discriminatory, or harmful views that would otherwise be seen as violations of rights.

            1.2.7.3.4.1.1. Pro:
            For example, refusing to provide services for LGBT+ individuals on the basis of exercising religious freedom.

      1.2.7.4. Pro:
      Organized religions have often sided with elites, legitimating or enhancing their power.

        1.2.7.4.1. Con:
        Religion has also fought against the political establishment.

          1.2.7.4.1.1. Pro:
          Jesus Christ was actually breaking social order [359].

          1.2.7.4.1.2. Pro:
          Faith based organizations have led or been a major part of social change [360], overthrowing injustices. e.g. ending slavery, civil rights.

            1.2.7.4.1.2.1. Con:
            Historically, slavery [361] has been supported by several major religions.

              1.2.7.4.1.2.1.1. Con:
              While Christianity [362] supported slavery in Roman and Medieval times, many prominent religious figures advocated for its abolition in the 18th century.

              1.2.7.4.1.2.1.2. Con:
              This is a non-unique argument. Almost everyone supported, contributed, or otherwise benefited from slavery until the English banned [363] it in 1833.

                1.2.7.4.1.2.1.2.1. Con:
                It disputes the continuous religious position, that they have they high ground.

              1.2.7.4.1.2.1.3. Con:
              Chattel slavery is contrary to Mosaic Law [364].

              1.2.7.4.1.2.1.4. Pro:
              The Bible [365] was used as an argument in favor of slavery [366].

                1.2.7.4.1.2.1.4.1. Con:
                Judeo-Christian religions commanded their followers to treat slaves fairly [367] and with respect [368]. Slavery was an ugly fact of life during the time, and religion helped make it less horrible.

                  1.2.7.4.1.2.1.4.1.1. Pro:
                  Under Judaeo-Christian teachings, slavery becomes a contractual agreement between an employer and an employee. A rather strict contract, but the idea is no different. Jewish [369] law [370] even prohibited buying and selling  kidnapped people [371] into slavery and returning escaped slaves [372]. Christian [373] teaching [374] placed even more emphasis on the fair treatment [375] of the slaves [376].

                1.2.7.4.1.2.1.4.2. Pro:
                Even if slavery in the Bible was different chronologically and geographically, American Christian slaveholders often quoted several pro-slavery passages from the Bible [377] to justify their practice.

                  1.2.7.4.1.2.1.4.2.1. Con:
                  The whole practice of slavery was different in comparison to American Christian slaveholders. Slaves working under Hebrews were not murdered, beaten or abused and if they were it was taboo.

                  1.2.7.4.1.2.1.4.2.2. Pro:
                  Christianity early on [378] did away with many social conventions and refused to assimilate into Roman culture. Before that the Jews often went into rebellion [379] and refused to assimilate into local culture. These two religions based on that track record could have banned slavery amongst at the very least its own ranks.

                1.2.7.4.1.2.1.4.3. Con:
                Jeremiah 34:17 [380] does explicitly condemn the practice of slavery, saying, "Therefore, thus says the Lord: You have not obeyed me by proclaiming liberty, every one to his brother and to his neighbor..."

              1.2.7.4.1.2.1.5. Con:
              Historically commerce (greed) supported, propagated, and grew slavery and exploitation - not religion.

            1.2.7.4.1.2.2. Pro:
            Faith was an important factor [381] in keeping the American Civil Rights movement together in the face of a concerted and powerful opposition.

              1.2.7.4.1.2.2.1. Con:
              It's impossible to know that people would not have stepped up to lead had churches not done so.

                1.2.7.4.1.2.2.1.1. Pro:

              1.2.7.4.1.2.2.2. Con:
              Many of the Churches themselves [383] did not participate, even as their congregations did.

              1.2.7.4.1.2.2.3. Con:
              The religious narrative instilled in the movement diminished the role of other oppressed groups that the religious did not like and cemented religious influence in the black community.

                1.2.7.4.1.2.2.3.1. Pro:
                Homophobia [384] may be more prevalent in the African American community because of religious influences.

                1.2.7.4.1.2.2.3.2. Pro:
                Certain components of religious practice, such as the attitude to money and education, can contribute to individuals remaining in or rising out of poverty. It is argued [385] that Conservative Protestantism may help perpetuate poverty.

                  1.2.7.4.1.2.2.3.2.1. Con:
                  The failure to provide reparations [386] for the stolen wealth of slavery is a far more convincing explanation as to why this community may struggle with poverty.

            1.2.7.4.1.2.3. Con:
            In religious societies, it makes sense that people who concern themselves with the big questions in life (which religions typically hold a monopoly over in religious societies), are also the ones who take big steps toward overthrowing injustices. It's correlation, not causation.

        1.2.7.4.2. Pro:
        In previous times monarchs derived the right to rule directly from the will of God. [387]

          1.2.7.4.2.1. Pro:

          1.2.7.4.2.2. Pro:

            1.2.7.4.2.2.1. Pro:
            Sweden funded the Church of Sweden, its own national church in 1477: royals and elected officials could only profess this faith. Although laws gradually allowed religious freedom, it was not until 1951 that nationals could leave the Church without having to be part of another congregation, and it was not until 2000 that the State and the Church were formally separated (Jänterä-Jareborg, p. 672 [389]).

            1.2.7.4.2.2.2. Pro:
            Until 2012 [390] the constitutional head of the Church of Norway [391] was the King of Norway.

  1.3. Pro:
  Religions are useful as sources of morality, spirituality, philosophy, and personal lessons.

    1.3.1. Pro:
    Our understanding of 'good' (and 'evil') is grounded in religious teachings and practice, meaning that without religion it would not be possible to claim that religion is either good or bad.

      1.3.1.1. Con:
      Other animals, particularly social species, provide evidence that moral and ethical behaviors are not exclusively human characteristics [392]. Irreligious people understand what is or is not good based upon their instincts as an empathizing social species.

      1.3.1.2. Con:
      This amounts to "we're-right-because-we-say-we're-right". It's evidence that religion is bad for humanity, because it amounts to letting the few tell the many what they have to do and what they can't do, under threat not just of being in the wrong but of eternal damnation.

      1.3.1.3. Con:
      If moral codes were universally originated in religion there would be more homogeneity between cultures and the various different religious denominations across the world.

      1.3.1.4. Pro:
      The very notion of "good" would not exist in the absence of religion, without a transcendent [393] instance, beyond human reach.

        1.3.1.4.1. Pro:
        Religion, and in particular the belief in a benign God, serves as a foundation for objective morality [394].

        1.3.1.4.2. Con:
        Good and bad are subjective terms that make up a dichotomy. They are often defined by each other, not by religion. Things are good only in comparison to bad things and vice versa. This does not require religion to work.

        1.3.1.4.3. Con:
        Since humans are in any case those who interpret and apply the transcendental notion of good, even if this is sanctioned by a higher power, its application is bound to abuse or misuse, in the same manner that any human construct on morality and ethics is.

          1.3.1.4.3.1. Con:
          Human judgement is indeed imperfect. Yet, the notion of good remains the same irrespective of any human judgement. This is why, for example, in Christian Bible it is said not to judge. This is the atribute of the divinity.

      1.3.1.5. Con:
      Perceptions of 'goodness' could have been derived naturally [395] or elsewhere, and then transcribed into religion.

      1.3.1.6. Con:
      Religious beliefs are full of contradictions, as are their teachings [396].

        1.3.1.6.1. Pro:
        The Bible's moral teachings are contradictory and inconsistent.

          1.3.1.6.1.1. Pro:
          The Bible concurrently commands [397] and forbids [398] the act of killing.

            1.3.1.6.1.1.1. Con:
            Contextual calls to kill in a war or to protect something or someone are not contradictory to a normative moral stance that one should not murder.

          1.3.1.6.1.2. Pro:
          The Bible simultaneously allows [399] for and forbids [400] slavery.

            1.3.1.6.1.2.1. Con:
            Slavery is a word that has a broad range of meaning [401] from kidnapping and forcing into servitude (which is prohibited in the Old and New Testament) to voluntary and/or temporary servitude which is permitted and regulated.

          1.3.1.6.1.3. Pro:
          The Bible claims that acts of goodness should be seen [402], and acts of goodness should be kept a secret [403], at the same time.

            1.3.1.6.1.3.1. Con:
            Matthew 5:16 [404] is in reference to persecution and acts of justice and mercy. Those are acts that require a subject (public). Acts kept in secret are acts of devotion and piety (private) kept secret to avoid hypocrisy and pride.

          1.3.1.6.1.4. Con:
          Commandments, interdictions and other normative claims do not make historical claims. Thus, issuing contradictory commands does not disqualify a document as historical.

          1.3.1.6.1.5. Con:
          Many historical documents put forward contradictory and inconsistent ideologies.

            1.3.1.6.1.5.1. Pro:
            Most professional historians would agree that the political writings of prominent Nazis are contradictory, inconsistent and incoherent [405]. They are nonetheless useful historical documents.

            1.3.1.6.1.5.2. Pro:
            While the Founding Fathers [406] of the United States fought for freedom, many of them showed how inconsistently they applied this belief through their support of slavery [407]. Their ideological inconsistencies do not make their accounts historically unimportant.

          1.3.1.6.1.6. Con:
          One does not have to agree or reconcile with the moral teachings that are prescribed by a historical document in order for the document to be considered a valid source of history.

          1.3.1.6.1.7. Pro:
          The Bible simultaneously approves [408] and forbids [409] lying.

            1.3.1.6.1.7.1. Con:
            I Kings 22 [410] does not approve lying. It describes how God orchestrated events through the false prophets. This passage does not work against the normative command not to bear a false witness.

        1.3.1.6.2. Pro:
        There are inconsistencies within the Qur'an.

          1.3.1.6.2.1. Pro:
          Chapter 42:51 [411] of the Qur'an says it is not fitting for Allah to speak directly to men but there are other instances where Allah does appear to speak directly to people, including to Moses in Chapter 4:164. [412]

          1.3.1.6.2.2. Con:
          Given that the ahadith of Prophet Muhammad explain the Qur'an and remove any ambiguities regarding Islamic doctrine [413], any inconsistencies within the Qur'an can be compared to ahadith with similar teachings to know which version stands.

            1.3.1.6.2.2.1. Con:
            In some fundamental areas, the Qur'an and ahadith are antithetical to one another [414]. This makes it more difficult to reconcile any contradictions within the Qur'an.

          1.3.1.6.2.3. Con:
          Inconsistencies within the Qur'an can be reconciled using the approach of al-Jam' (p. 227 [415]), which is widely used in Islam to reconcile contradictory ahadith (prophetic narrations).

            1.3.1.6.2.3.1. Pro:
            Under Al-Jam’ [416], the second narration which seems contradictory is raised and adopted to give a correct understanding. This ensures that two narrations no longer contradict each other (p. 228).

          1.3.1.6.2.4. Con:
          According to the scholar Ibn Hazm, any contradictions that we find in the Qur'an reflect "the state of our [human] knowledge limitations [417]" and are not actual contradictions.

          1.3.1.6.2.5. Pro:
          There are inconsistencies about how man was created with 96:2 [418] saying man was created from a blood clot, 25:54 [419] saying he was created from water, and 15:26 [420] saying man was moulded from clay.

          1.3.1.6.2.6. Pro:
          Chapter 39:4 [421] states that Allah could have a son if he wished, while Chapter 6:101 [422] says he could not have a son as he had no consort.

        1.3.1.6.3. Con:
        Often religious contradictions create puzzles that convey important lessons once they are solved or considered carefully.

    1.3.2. Con:
    Religion is not the only source of ethics, secular ethics [423] exists and can be used to evaluate whether or not religion is good this is not a contradiction.

    1.3.3. Pro:
    Religions can establish particularly strong, rules-based, moral codes for people to live by.

      1.3.3.1. Pro:
      For centuries, religions provided society with common behavioral guidelines, enabling organized and peaceful coexistence, just as constitutions and legal codes do today.

        1.3.3.1.1. Con:
        History has been quite the opposite of peaceful [424] for virtually all of recorded human existence. Peaceful coexistence has simply not been a feature of reality.

          1.3.3.1.1.1. Pro:
          Human history has largely been characterized by ignorance, war, murder, theft, and bigotry, and other crimes rather than peaceful coexistence.

        1.3.3.1.2. Pro:
        Although religions are not perfect, the order that they bring is preferable to the disorder that would ensue in their absence.

          1.3.3.1.2.1. Pro:
          There is some evidence that Druids [425], religious practitioners in ancient Celtic societies, would also resolve disputes.

          1.3.3.1.2.2. Con:
          A prison is a structure too and religion isn't so different: Electrified bars made of peer pressure, surrounded by an ocean of hell, with your soul manacled to the floor, and a lock made of belief.

        1.3.3.1.3. Pro:
        All major religions have a core morality [426] that is summed up in treating others in the way that you want to be treated.

        1.3.3.1.4. Pro:
        Religion improved the strength [427] of groups to outcompete other groups.

          1.3.3.1.4.1. Pro:
          Shared religions with "big gods" that see everything and punish moral transgressions in the afterlife was initially needed to achieve trust between strangers who would otherwise cheat and exploit each other. This makes larger societies possible [428], causing such religions to spread and gain dominance through "cultural evolution" [429].

            1.3.3.1.4.1.1. Con:
            Trusting and social [430] behavior [431] is seen in animals who presumably have no gods.

            1.3.3.1.4.1.2. Con:
            Human settlements grew into civilizations because of agriculture [432]. Why they settled down from being nomadic tribes is up for debate but they were already living in social groups without developing into large societies until the development of agriculture.

            1.3.3.1.4.1.3. Pro:
            One of the goals of religion isn't to make you good but to remind you that you can be. If we agree on this it means that humans can also be bad. If the counter to 'bad' people in a state is the court and police. Then for the world it is God.

              1.3.3.1.4.1.3.1. Con:
              Suppose every country ceded its sovereignty to the UN and they drew up a common set of laws for us all. Then the counter to all bad people would be court. The fact that the legal systems are separate does not mean that there is a god above them.

              1.3.3.1.4.1.3.2. Con:
              Since crime is prevalent all over the world it would appear that God is not an effective counter to bad people in this world.

            1.3.3.1.4.1.4. Con:
            Inventions by prehistoric hunter-gatherer societies (tools and curing technologies) were innovations increasing the stability/well-being of all tribe members. There is no evidence for a key role of religious values in these societies; basic human needs and the objective benefits of cooperation can account for these innovations.

          1.3.3.1.4.2. Con:
          -> See 1.3.3.1.4.1.2.

          1.3.3.1.4.3. Con:
          This is classic "others must fail" thinking. How would a religious group outperform the non-religious? By ostracizing or killing them or forcing them to comply. The fact that there was a victor does not mean that overall efficiency was raised.

            1.3.3.1.4.3.1. Pro:
            Relates perfectly to "the survival of the fittest", the backbone of human nature forcing the weak to submit to the powerful religion rather than their preferred.

          1.3.3.1.4.4. Con:
          Competition is a good thing where it leads to progress, but religions do not change so when a religious group wins progress is less likely.

            1.3.3.1.4.4.1. Con:
            Religions are constantly changing. The rate of change is slow, but that is a positive because it forces new ideas to be tested sufficiently before the new ideas are accepted.

          1.3.3.1.4.5. Pro:
          From caveman rituals [433] to the worship of the Pharaoh [434] as a man God of ancient Egypt, religion as a whole has evolved throughout history. Rituals are a cornerstone of human conduct and help bind together a society.

          1.3.3.1.4.6. Pro:
          If religion was just a by-product of human's characteristics, it would not have become the dominant organizing system of every society on earth. This demonstrates that it was a useful adaptation [435], and not a maladaption (at least at the time).

            1.3.3.1.4.6.1. Pro:
            Researchers consider that prosocial religions proliferated through “cultural diffusion, population expansions, and conquest” as they managed to forge “anonymous strangers into large, cohesive moral communities tied together with the sacred bonds of a common supernatural jurisdiction” (Norenzayan, p. 9 [436]).

            1.3.3.1.4.6.2. Pro:
            Despite predictions of religions' demise in the last centuries, most humans in the world are still religious and religions have been growing and mutating at a quick pace (Norenzayan, p. 2) [437].

              1.3.3.1.4.6.2.1. Pro:
              Estimates calculate that there are about 10,000 religions in the world, with most people adhering to a low number of big religions. Worldly, there are 2 billions of self-identified Christians and 1.3 people identify as Muslims (Norenzayan, p.2 [438]).

              1.3.3.1.4.6.2.2. Con:
              In Australia, religiously unaffiliated [439] people have increased from less than 1% in 1966, to over 30% in 2016.

                1.3.3.1.4.6.2.2.1. Con:
                This only applies to Australia, not the world.

              1.3.3.1.4.6.2.3. Con:
              There is only one religion whose percentage share of the population is expected to grow [440] and that is Islam. This is only because Muslims have the highest fertility rate [441] of any religious group and is far above the average rate of the world population.

              1.3.3.1.4.6.2.4. Con:
              In Europe, the number of non-religious citizens, especially among young adults, is increasing [442].

            1.3.3.1.4.6.3. Con:
            While some religion may have thrived and survived as they provided advantages (social cohesion, cooperation, rules), some present-day societies are successful at “sustaining large-scale cooperation with institutions such as court, police and mechanisms for enforcing contracts” (Norenzayan, p.9 [443]).

              1.3.3.1.4.6.3.1. Pro:
              In Scandinavian countries, strong institutions that facilitate cooperation “usurped” the community-building functions of religion, creating societies that are “some of the most cooperative, peaceful, and prosperous in the world” and thus making religion useless (Norenzayan, p.9) [444].

              1.3.3.1.4.6.3.2. Con:
              The fact that other systems can, through reason or otherwise, replace religion, doesn't mean that religion hasn't been, up until then, the dominant and successful adaptation.

              1.3.3.1.4.6.3.3. Con:
              The long-term effects of such replacements are not clear and there are examples of dismantling religion in communist countries which, at least partially, led to undesireable long-term consequences.

                1.3.3.1.4.6.3.3.1. Con:

        1.3.3.5.4. Con:

    1.3.4. Con:
    There is no value that religion teaches that cannot be found in philosophy, politics, and psychology.

      1.3.4.1. Pro:
      Using a utilitarian approach [547] encourages people to think about the costs and benefits of their actions in a way that a rules-based framework provided by a religion does not.

      1.3.4.2. Con:
      At the core of any moral philosophy is the faith that whichever you conform to is grounded in a metaphysical understanding of human nature. If that core of human nature, that holy spirit, was not there we would be unable to speak on the good.

        1.3.4.2.1. Con:
        Secular ethics [548] is a topic in philosophy and is not based on faith. There are many different versions none of which are based on faith.

          1.3.4.2.1.1. Pro:
          Utilitarianism [549] is not based on faith.

          1.3.4.2.1.2. Pro:
          Kantian Deontology [550] is not based on faith.

          1.3.4.2.1.3. Pro:
          Ethical intuitionism [551] is not based on faith.

          1.3.4.2.1.4. Pro:
          All forms of moral relativism [552] are not based in faith.

            1.3.4.2.1.4.1. Con:
            If someone has no faith in their moral convictions, how can they say they are true? Moral relativism is grounded in the idea that we cannot know what is good and therefore does not speak on the good or of it.

              1.3.4.2.1.4.1.1. Con:
              The point of Relativism is to judge actions as good or bad based on the context of the society in which one lives. It is an inherently subjective framework but nonetheless enables moral judgements to take place.

      1.3.4.3. Con:
      There are few questions that only religion can answer and not any other philosophy. For example, questions regarding existence or death.

        1.3.4.3.1. Con:
        Religion does not have answers to these questions either it just makes up speculative answers with no justification at all.

        1.3.4.3.2. Pro:
        The answer that religion provides are based on beliefs and opinions but an answer nonetheless for the faithful.

          1.3.4.3.2.1. Con:
          A so called answer which is no more than a speculative guess is useless and can serve to encourage people to not search for the true answer thus stifling curiosity and progress.

        1.3.4.3.3. Pro:
        Religion provides for objective meaning and an answer to the question of why we exist, which is a deep psychological need. How we exist or what exists for which science suggests answers can never meet that psychological need.

        1.3.4.3.4. Con:
        Philosophy answers to the question of why we exist no different than religion. It also explores death and the afterlife. There is no evidence that these fields are only available to organized belief systems.

      1.3.4.4. Con:
      The absence of religion has led people to seek solace in consumer culture rather than spirituality.

        1.3.4.4.1. Con:

        1.3.4.4.2. Con:

          1.3.4.4.2.1. Con:

      1.3.4.5. Pro:

In cultural relativism people decide together what is considered acceptable and moral behavior allowing society to freely change with the times.

        1.3.4.5.1. Con:

    1.3.5. Con:
    Religion does not rely on evidence and logic to draw conclusions.

      1.3.5.1. Con:
      There have been great religious philosophers who used logic and evidence to draw conclusions, evaluate their religion, and support reason.

        1.3.5.1.1. Con:
        It's plausible that each of these great philosophers did not truly believe, instead choosing to conform only to avoid trouble, and remained unhindered by drawing unfounded assumptions.

        1.3.5.1.2. Con:
        This leaves out the not-so-great philosophers who reject logic and evidence to draw conclusion, in favor of their religion.

          1.3.5.1.2.1. Pro:
          For example, Al-Ghazali [553], who basically made the case that philosophy [554] didn't work because it didn't show faith to be reasonable and failed to prove the existence of God.

            1.3.5.1.2.1.1. Pro:

            1.3.5.1.2.1.2. Pro:
            Orientalist [557] scholars have argued that Al-Ghazali caused a decline in scientific advancement in Islam.

        1.3.5.1.3. Pro:
        -> See 1.3.3.4.1.3.3.

        1.3.5.1.4. Pro:
        Nearly all the founders of modern science were Christians. These include men such as Kepler [558], Boyle [559], Pascal [560], Pasteur [561], Newton [562], etc.

          1.3.5.1.4.1. Con:
          Christianity only tolerated their scientific work on the premise that it did not conflict Christianity. The moment they discovered or hypothesized anything contrary to Christian doctrine, religious authorities would do pretty much anything to step in the way of their ability to do science.

          1.3.5.1.4.2. Con:
          Also, nearly all of them, included the ones listed, were white and male. Following this logic would imply that being white, male and religious makes you a good scientist.

          1.3.5.1.4.3. Con:
          Without illustrating that Christianity specifically motivated and provoked their pursuit of science and rationality simply listing founding scientists who happen to be Christians is not evidence for anything.

        1.3.5.1.5. Con:
        Rationalism long predates [563] Christianity. Philosophical and scientific inquiry began long before Christianity.

          1.3.5.1.5.1. Pro:
          Considering the major influence [564] of Hellenistic and Roman philosophers (including Socrates, Plato and Aristotle) on medieval Christian thinkers, it can be argued that non-religious ancient [565] philosophers were actually more seminal to Western philosophy than those who applied Christian doctrines to previously-constructed theories.

      1.3.5.2. Con:
      Believing without evidence is not always bad; faith can lead to beneficial outcomes.

        1.3.5.2.1. Con:
        The knock on effect in someone's life of deciding that they can draw conclusions without solid evidence can undermine their life bringing chaos through ignorance rather than order through investigation.

        1.3.5.2.2. Pro:
        -> See 1.2.7.4.1.2.2.

        1.3.5.2.3. Pro:

          1.3.5.2.3.1. Con:
          The monarchy of England was eventually restored and the leader of the rebellion Oliver Cromwell [567] effectively made himself a dictator. This is not the kind of example to be used as a plus mark for religion. This is especially the case given that the monarchs were head of the church of England [568].

        1.3.5.2.4. Con:
        This might be true when there is not enough evidence available. However, the habit of believing without relying on evidence, which may be more common among the religious, makes it socially acceptable for people to ignore available evidence in favor of religious dogma.

        1.3.5.2.5. Con:
        Getting hit by a car may have beneficial outcomes if the victim gets a massive settlement or if they somehow meet their future spouse as a result. This doesn't justify trying to get hit by a car or thinking it is not bad.

        1.3.5.2.6. Con:
        Statistical coincidence ensures that sometimes believing without evidence is not bad, but it still usually is.

      1.3.5.3. Pro:
      Religion creates a fantasy supposedly coexisting with reality. Then people prioritize this fantasy over reality in making decisions.

      1.3.5.4. Pro:
      Many religions make unsubstantiated, unverifiable claims about death and what happens to us after it.

        1.3.5.4.1. Pro:
        This creates intellectual apathy in addressing problems by attaching a person's interest in life after death/paradise to maintaining their faith.

      1.3.5.5. Pro:

        1.3.5.5.1. Pro:
        Religion is permissive towards irrational or superstitious thinking and behavior, which then spread to domains outside religion, such as politics.

Thus, it would not satisfy a nihlist who was looking for some reason why life had value, since the religious justification depends on an unproven notion of God and his or her actions.

    1.3.7. Pro:
    Spirituality is a part of who we are as humans, and shared spirituality with common understanding (religion) is the way it gets expressed in community.

      1.3.7.1. Con:
      Spirituality in its traditional meaning is religious - belief in the supernatural and/or unprovable - and this is not 'part of who we are' for all humans. It is not required to have this to be a human.

      1.3.7.2. Con:
      'Shared spirituality with common meaning' is not the only definition of religion. Modern meanings of 'spirituality' include references to human emotion and wonder at the universe. These things are not supernatural, and therefore not religious in nature, meaning spirituality is not necessarily religious.

      1.3.7.3. Pro:
      Religion can be viewed as a shared and negotiated spirituality.

        1.3.7.3.1. Pro:
        Much like language: creole languages [619], stable natural languages developed from a mixture of different languages to become a native language, are negotiated.

        1.3.7.3.2. Con:
        "Religion [620]" comes from Latin and suggests a commitment, either written or spoken, to a group or cannon, that would be binding and to that extent non-negotiable.

        1.3.7.3.3. Con:
        The Gospel of Thomas [621], logion 114, shows that mithraic christianity has been gender politics, for the most part, for the last 2000 years.

        1.3.7.3.4. Con:
        Religions are originally dictated by their god(s) through the religious books. The word of God is not negotiable

          1.3.7.3.4.1. Con:
          Gods are human creations that represents archetypes and role models which survived multiple generations through storytelling.

            1.3.7.3.4.1.1. Con:
            Even if we were to assume that gods were only human creations, the words of the religious books exists and their commandments are empowered by the religious entities.

            1.3.7.3.4.1.2. Con:
            There is no irrefutable evidence that God or gods are merely human creation. The limits of the current technology and the constancy of change of human understanding of reality is not capable of supporting generalized statement pertaining to the existence or non-existence of God.

          1.3.7.3.4.2. Pro:
          Younger generations do not feel like they are part of the negotiation, because most religions were settled before they were born.

          1.3.7.3.4.3. Pro:
          Many people believe that God actually exist, and decide to follow the religious dogma without negotiation.

          1.3.7.3.4.4. Con:
          Religious texts reflect the meditative experiences of prophets or messiahs, which they then interpret and articulate. This process is subject to human failings; in this sense, scriptures are negotiable.

            1.3.7.3.4.4.1. Pro:
            The Judaic Midrash [622] is a body of rabbinic literature in which rabbis interpret and comment on the Torah and other religious laws in order to resolve problems and negotiate a common understanding.

              1.3.7.3.4.4.1.1. Pro:
              Contemporary [623] forms of 'midrash' include literature and artwork to facilitate the interpretation of sacred texts. According to some scholars 'midrash' can also be used to “update” or “enhance” sacred texts, thus being a form of rewriting them in a way that  makes them "more acceptable to later ethical standards" and renders what is less obviously implausible.

            1.3.7.3.4.4.2. Con:
            The texts are considered to be the Word of God (to the prophet/author) by the followers of the religion, therefore, they are not negotiable.

        1.3.7.3.5. Con:
        Even leaders of many religions will tell you that what people get from their religion will vary from person to person. Thus viewing religion as a shared spirituality is an inaccurate perception.

      1.3.7.4. Con:
      Organized religions discourage personal exploration and spirituality.

        1.3.7.4.1. Pro:
        The politics of religion have obscured and diminished the development of collective and personal spirituality.

          1.3.7.4.1.1. Pro:

            1.3.7.4.1.1.1. Pro:
            These power struggles between high hierarchical figures divide believers and split the faith.

          1.3.7.4.1.2. Pro:
