            1.3.7.4.1.2.1. Pro:
            -> See 1.3.6.3.5.6.1.

            1.3.7.4.1.2.2. Con:
            A dogma is only a marker for what constitutes a necessary belief in any given system. You can't be a Christian who don't believe that Jesus is the son of God, because believing that is what constitutes being a Christian. Likewise, you can't be an atheist who believes in God, because not believing in God is the sole criterion for being an atheist. Dogma does not block spiritual development, it merely categorises it.

            1.3.7.4.1.2.3. Pro:
            There are virtually an infinite number of ways for spiritual development (each person can have his own). When following one specific dogma, you can't explore the other ways and can't decide by your own experience which one is best for you.

              1.3.7.4.1.2.3.1. Con:
              An infinite number of ways is more ways than a person has time to explore in a lifetime, religion narrows the field to a smaller subset of infinite ways, which are still more ways than a person can explore in their life.

              1.3.7.4.1.2.3.2. Con:
              The point of religion is to show and teach people the "correct" way (whatever each religion feels the correct way is). Allowing individualism runs counter to this, as it is by definition, not the "correct" way.

                1.3.7.4.1.2.3.2.1. Con:
                Religion and individualism are not mutually exclusive

                1.3.7.4.1.2.3.2.2. Pro:
                Thousand of years of developing a particular theory will most of the time have more wisdom and useful knowledge than what a single individual can produce.

              1.3.7.4.1.2.3.3. Pro:
              Spiritual development is never a collective task. The collective task should be supporting the spiritual development of its members. Religion is actually a tool to sanction spiritual development as it establishes its boundaries.

            1.3.7.4.1.2.4. Pro:
            Faith is a closed-ended inquiry. No learning is possible if you already decided the answer beforehand.

            1.3.7.4.1.2.5. Con:
            Religious dogma can be used as structure for building a stable and fulfilled life. There is nothing that says dogma is necessarily enforced top-down.

              1.3.7.4.1.2.5.1. Con:
              Most organized religions [625] are to some degree [626] hierarchical [627] and dogma is indeed enforced top-down.

          1.3.7.4.1.3. Pro:
          Religion has killed the faith. God and Satan are from within a person. All her/his good traits are God and bad ones are Satan. But religions have taken this fact out by personifying God and Satan it has taken the introspective improvement out of the mankind.

            1.3.7.4.1.3.1. Con:
            But by externalising these values and behaviours to archetypal deities, not only they were in a better place to debate about them and come to consensus, but they made these ideals memorable and easily transmittable through stories, myths and folklore.

        1.3.7.4.2. Pro:
        Most religions are limited to obsolete philosophical and spiritual concepts that contradict a modern understanding of existence.

          1.3.7.4.2.1. Pro:
          Monotheism was a theosophical revolution in reaction to polytheism but it may be irrelevant today to anthropomorphize what justifies our existence. en.wikipedia.org [628]

          1.3.7.4.2.2. Con:
          If a particular religion is true, that religion would provide incalculable benefit to society by conveying true information to people about transcendent reality, morality, and salvation.

            1.3.7.4.2.2.1. Con:
            Religion can never really be proven to be true and is ultimately based on belief [629], which the state of mind in which a person thinks something to be the case with or without there being empirical evidence to prove it.

              1.3.7.4.2.2.1.1. Pro:
              Most religions rely on supernatural causes [630], which cannot be empirically verified.

            1.3.7.4.2.2.2. Con:
            As no religion is yet proven true, it cannot be stated that they provided true information about reality, morality and salvation. If a religion is proven true in the future, then they will indeed provide incalculable benefit, but until then, that is not clear.

            1.3.7.4.2.2.3. Pro:
            In fact they all might be true to some degree, and in that lead people to better understanding of the transcendent.

          1.3.7.4.2.3. Pro:
          Religious "truths" have been carved in stone (e.g. in the Bible, Qur'an, Torah, etc.), so when there are conflicts between these truths, they cannot be debated.

        1.3.7.4.3. Pro:
        In order to spread religion among the masses, organized religion has reduced subtle human pursuits such as "introspection" and "spirituality" into ritualistic activities to be performed mechanically, and sometimes performed purely to avoid committing "sin".

          1.3.7.4.3.1. Con:
          Introspection being mechanical shouldn't actually be possible. Some religions may make spiritual pursuits ritualistic, but not all do, and few originally intended to. The Christian faith, for example, was initially intended as a vibrant personal relationship.

            1.3.7.4.3.1.1. Con:
            It's inferred that it's the attempt that is mechanical and that a positive result is less likely when that is the case, not only because the attempt is by rote, but because the religion may be a distraction rather than an aid.

          1.3.7.4.3.2. Pro:
          Repeating the same mantras can't be a good start for true introspection.

            1.3.7.4.3.2.1. Con:
            Mantras are used as a focus tool for introspection. They are used to negate extroversive noise and allow for "true introspection."

          1.3.7.4.3.3. Pro:
          Delaying introspection until particular times of day can't be good for the process. The impact of earlier occurrences will have dulled.

          1.3.7.4.3.4. Pro:
          Praying to a god is by definition not introspection. It's either referring to an outside being or to just a part of oneself, depending on your religion.

            1.3.7.4.3.4.1. Con:
            Referring to a part of oneself is the same as introspection.

          1.3.7.4.3.5. Con:
          Prayer, mantras, meditation, etc... are used to inspire positive action as much as they may be to avoid negative action (sin).

          1.3.7.4.3.6. Con:
          The power of ritual should not be discounted.

        1.3.7.4.4. Pro:
        We need to distinguish between a spiritual belief (a mythology) and a religion. There is nothing wrong with having a spiritual belief. All religions are actually nothing more than the business organisation and exploitation of people with spiritual beliefs. Therefore religion has never been a good thing for humanity.

        1.3.7.4.5. Con:
        Organized religion can guide you on the path of spirituality. It creates the space (e.g. church), the resources (e.g. theology) and the time (e.g. prayer, confession) to exercise and explore your spirituality.

          1.3.7.4.5.1. Con:
          Organized religion also restricts your ability to think outside of the dogma imposed by that religion.

          1.3.7.4.5.2. Con:
          Setting oneself on the path of spirituality is not necessarily something positive.

        1.3.7.4.6. Con:
        Religion has become a link to spirituality. Some without it will never experience that dimension.

          1.3.7.4.6.1. Con:
          Spirituality is unnecessary.

          1.3.7.4.6.2. Con:
          Spirituality is not a well defined concept. What a religious person considers spiritual may well be no different from the awe and wonder an atheist might experience.

            1.3.7.4.6.2.1. Con:
            Except that for a religious person awe and wonder could be all in his or her head, while the atheist may find it in real things, like a tree or particle physics.

        1.3.7.4.7. Con:
        Even if organized religions were limiting space for spirituality seeking, since the very term "spirituality" is poorly defined and arguably misguiding, with questionable benefits, this limitation is not necessarily something bad.

        1.3.7.4.8. Con:
        Many organized religions encourage their adepts [631] to seek spirituality.

          1.3.7.4.8.1. Con:
          Organized religion limits spirituality to their own version: There is no seeking for it, just reading and listening until the reader/audience conflates the moral tales and truisms with the "facts" and the tenets.

            1.3.7.4.8.1.1. Con:
            Doctrines of organized religions do not necessarily require uniformity of the spiritual paths.

          1.3.7.4.8.2. Pro:
          Many organized religions argue that their practices allow adherents to achieve a greater level [632] of spirituality [633].

        1.3.7.4.9. Pro:
        Religion has made widespread the idea that spirituality is connected to institutions or communities when in fact it is an individual experience. This has diminished the value of spirituality on individual levels.

          1.3.7.4.9.1. Pro:
          Personal exploration does not come from a group.

        1.3.7.4.10. Pro:
        All the benefits from having a religion can be had with a personal or communal spiritual exploration, without the disadvantages of dogmas and oppressive institutions.

        1.3.7.4.11. Pro:
        Religions often hold that there is some higher force or forces that protect living things and uphold moral order, which reduces our sense of responsibility for our actions.

          1.3.7.4.11.1. Pro:
          The diffusion of responsibility [634], a psychological phenomenon in which people are less likely to take action, may affect the sense of responsibility of those belonging to religious groups as they may feel someone else (God) will take care of situations.

          1.3.7.4.11.2. Con:
          Some religions, like Christianity, endorse personal responsibility [635] and claim that people's acts in life determine their future in the afterlife.

            1.3.7.4.11.2.1. Pro:
            In monotheistic religions, the intent of the deity is primarily benevolent. It is well known to those who practice these religions that what happens on this planet is indeed important, and that we are capable [636] and responsible [637] creatures.

            1.3.7.4.11.2.2. Con:
            Many religions express a sense of fatalism [638] in which humans' destinies are almost predetermined (and dependent on Karma, God's plan, etc.)

            1.3.7.4.11.2.3. Con:
            Some branches of Christianity do in fact believe god has a plan for each individual. Calvinism [639] for example believes in predestination.

          1.3.7.4.11.3. Pro:
          Lack of responsibility promoted by religion leads to inaction on major issues such as climate change [640].

          1.3.7.4.11.4. Con:
          Some religions, such as Buddhism, Jainism, Confucianism, and Daoism, are nontheistic [641] and therefore would not suggest this.

  1.4. Pro:
  Religion helps people come together in order to create networks or long lasting relationships.

    1.4.1. Con:
    Social networks and communities can create echo chambers [642], wherein individuals and groups no longer consider or even perceive opposing views.

      1.4.1.1. Pro:
      Individuals often join and participate in groups that share their existing beliefs, leading to the reinforcement of group identity and a stronger resistance to opposing perspectives.

    1.4.2. Pro:
    Most religions hold peace and tolerance as core values and promote love and respect towards others.

      1.4.2.1. Pro:
      -> See 1.3.3.5.3.2.2.

      1.4.2.2. Con:
      While it may have fostered unity amongst believers, religion has historically caused more division, conflict and violence than unity between groups.

        1.4.2.2.1. Pro:
        Historically most religions have spread a message of love and respect for others so long as they adhere to the same religion. The idea of tolerance has been taken up by some religions since crusades, stonings, and burning witches went out of style, but tolerance implies superiority, which implies a lack of respect.

        1.4.2.2.2. Pro:
        These communities tend to work well in creating social bonds between its members, but often also facilitate closed-mindedness or lack of curiosity towards non-members, by forming a common basis of judgment, resulting in the groups's seclusion. Isolated individuals are then often pressurized to assimilate in the belief system before social affirmation can occur.

        1.4.2.2.3. Pro:
        Religions tend to instill prejudice in their followers.

          1.4.2.2.3.1. Pro:

            1.4.2.2.3.1.1. Pro:
            In recent decades the favourite word which religious leaders use to suggest they're not bigots is a giveaway that that's exactly what many of them are: They claim they "tolerate", a word that's open to interpretation either as biased (to placate their bigots) or unbiased (to placate everyone else).

              1.4.2.2.3.1.1.1. Pro:

            Many potential marriages have been stopped dead by differences of religion.

        1.4.2.2.5. Pro:
        Religious people tend to believe that they have a purpose in life, that they do the right thing, behave in the correct way and have ethical superiority which creates division, criticism and hate.

      1.4.2.3. Pro:
      Islamic scriptures [650] and teachings ask followers not to persecute other faiths.

        1.4.2.3.1. Pro:

          The quoted examples include the way in which Muslims entered Jerusalem peacefully, yet it also contrasts that with the slaughter perpetrated by Crusaders [652] in 1099 [653]. Therefore, while one religion was peaceful in that case, another one was far from peaceful.

            1.4.2.3.1.2.1. Pro:
            The peaceful coexistence between Muslims and Christians in Spain was stopped by the Reconquista [656], “a continuous phenomenon by which the Christian Iberian kingdoms opposed and conquered the Muslim kingdoms” for about 780 years, culminating in 1492.

    1.4.3. Pro:
    Religious beliefs/interpretations have been responsible for the strengthening of communities and social bonds, reducing the levels of division within in-groups/those with similar beliefs. These common ideals and values have knit people together for centuries.

      1.4.3.1. Con:
      Deeming people of the same religion as not following the imposed prescriptions strictly enough often served as casus belli, even when the real motives were of different kind. Such sorts of war justifications were often practiced, e.g. by Saladin [662].

      1.4.3.2. Pro:
      Having a shared religion, and therefore a shared worldview, contributes to the trust and coherence within communities.

      1.4.3.3. Pro:
      The larger social units enabled by fictional religious ideas [663] are an essential component of the expansion and dominance of humans over other hominid species.

        1.4.3.3.1. Pro:
        Regrouping behind common archetypal ideals [664] increases coordination, cooperation and chances for survival and reproduction.

          1.4.3.3.1.1. Con:
          Religion fragments the commonality of each state and other organisations to those who are of the religion and those who are not, thus reducing coordination and cooperation. For instance there are multiple examples of religious groups not cooperating with local law enforcement or defying local law, which has been known to result in loss of life or liberty.

        1.4.3.3.2. Pro:

          1.4.3.3.2.1. Con:

    1.4.4. Con:

          1.4.4.2.2.1. Pro:

            1.4.4.2.2.1.1. Con:
            Religions focus too much on their differences thus making it difficult to have a common understanding with one another.

            1.4.4.2.2.1.2. Con:

            1.4.4.2.2.1.3. Con:
            Long periods of harmony have been used an excuse for wrongdoing for as long as there's been wrongdoing. "She's a pillar of the community" "It's his first offence" "She runs a charity" "He employs thousands of people". They don't mean anything. The victim is still dead, ostracized or mutilated.

            Tolerance is claimed to take the high ground. Not because the religion doesn't believe that it's better. It's lipstick on a pig. Laws are obeyed only where the authorities have the power to implement the laws. Where religion can get away with stuff it tries, because it believes it's right.

For instance where before we may have had two countries, say Blebria and Contabra, now we have religious Blebrians, atheist Blebrians, religious Contabrans and atheist Contabrans. That amounts to 6 divisions where we had only nationalism, and some of those divisions are more severe, for instance between the religious Blebrians and the atheist Contabrans.

    1.4.5. Con:
    Community cohesion can be achieved without belief in an omnipresent or omnipotent deity.

      1.4.5.1. Pro:

      1.4.5.2. Pro:
      Secular spaces such as pubs, schools, neighborhood associations, civic events, and parks do this as well.

      1.4.5.3. Pro:

        1.5.2.6.6. Pro:

          1.5.2.6.6.1. Pro:

          1.5.2.6.6.2. Con:
          This is not true of all religions. Christian apologetics [825], for example, specifically focuses on real, falsifiable evidence, and many other religions do likewise.

            1.5.2.6.6.2.1. Con:
            Apologetics does not imply that a religion is evidence based, only that even if you start with an unfounded assumption you can draw similarly unfounded conclusions from those assumptions in a logical manner that is, nevertheless, built on feet of clay. For instance if a blind man presumes that all birds only have one wing, he can deduce that birds fly in intricate patterns to get to where they intend. The process of deduction is logical but as the assumption is unfounded so is the conclusion.

          1.5.2.6.6.3. Con:
          Many religious beliefs are likely to have been derived by practicality, like not eating the meat of unclean animals. Without knowing the proper way to cook pork the risk of parasite infection was higher, so that belief indeed has a practical base.

        1.5.2.6.7. Pro:
        Evidence [826] can be tested; its truth can be determined by the consistency of the results. There is no religious evidence that consistently provides the same result; therefore, the evidence is not reliable and should not be trusted.

      According to research [827], "religion is most able to thrive in seriously dysfunctional societies".

      1.5.3.3. Pro:
      The world’s most religious [828] countries are in very low positions in the Human Development Index [829], while the least religious countries are "very high" or "high" in the same index.

        1.5.3.3.1. Pro:
        Statistics [830] suggest some level of correlation between religiosity and lower human development rates.

        1.5.3.3.2. Con:
        The countries that are very high in the index got there through an extended period of Christian culture. Recent decline in Christian belief has come through materialism, consumerism, individualism, so they are living off spiritual capital in a sense.

          1.5.3.3.2.1. Con:
          Industrial Revolution [831] started and took pace in a period of religious skepticism [832], with less religiously restrictive countries decisively outperforming the more religious ones.

          1.5.3.3.2.2. Con:
          Correlation without causation. Arguably gun powder had a more profound effect on the economic well being of many countries since it enabled them to colonize other parts of the world. The industrial revolution had an even higher impact Christianity simply had nothing to do with their economic success.

          1.5.3.3.2.3. Con:
          Possibly an effect rather than a cause. Christian countries were fairly hard on others for significant periods, for instance the Crusades [833], the Spanish Inquisition [834].

      1.5.3.4. Con:
      Without some way to reliably measure "religiosity" it is impossible to state whether societies are more, or less, religious given their economic status.

        1.5.3.4.1. Con:
        Gallup [835] and Pew [836] polls, for instances, are made with large samples of respondents in each country who are asked if religion is important for them (or in their lives). Self-report surveys may be limited as research tools, but they do provide some reliable data and are used in a huge variety of fields.

      1.5.3.5. Pro:
      -> See 1.5.1.6.2.2.

      1.5.3.6. Con:
      Even if societal downfall and religion are positively correlated, that does not prove that there is a causal connection between religion and social downfall.

        1.5.3.6.1. Pro:
        People living in poor and in conflict areas tend to be religious, in part because they seek the solace of religion. They are not unsuccessful because they are religious, they are religious because they are unsuccessful.

          1.5.3.6.1.1. Con:
          -> See 1.5.1.6.2.2.

          1.5.3.6.1.2. Pro:
          Religious groups aiding poor countries may potentially sway that poor community to follow said religion. The correlation doesn't mean religion is making countries poor, it can be poor communities leading to religion.

        1.5.3.6.2. Con:
        -> See 1.5.3.2.

        1.5.3.6.3. Pro:
        It is difficult to determine if societies are more economically successful because they are less religious or societies are less religious because they are more economically successful. The negative correlation between economic success and religiosity does not favor one conclusion over the other.

          1.5.3.6.3.1. Pro:
          Correlation does not imply causation.

          1.5.3.6.3.2. Con:
          One way or the other, there seems to be a clear link between development and religiosity and it is clear that religiosity fares better when a country is less developed.

          1.5.3.6.3.3. Con:
          -> See 1.5.1.6.2.2.1.

        1.5.3.6.4. Con:
        -> See 1.5.1.6.2.2.

    1.5.4. Con:
    Christianity has been integral to Western civilization.

      1.5.4.1. Pro:
      Western Civilization via Christianity has given the world a great educational system.

        1.5.4.1.1. Con:
        The earliest forms of formal schooling [837] were developed in ancient Egypt and Mesopotamia.

        1.5.4.1.2. Con:
        City-states in ancient Greece [838] provided private education in literacy, arts and gymnastics for young boys (from 7 to 14 years old) mimicking modern formal schooling way before Christianity.

        1.5.4.1.3. Con:
        Ancient Greece [839] also developed forms of higher education where rich students could contniue their studies, learning rhetoric, mathematics, geography, natural history, politics, and logic.

          1.5.4.1.3.1. Pro:
          In fact, the Hellenistic influence in modern education can be traced in the word Lyceum [840], Aristotle's school named after its location, and a current terminology that many Western and Eastern countries still use.

        1.5.4.1.4. Pro:
        Medieval universities started as Christian [841] monastic schools and modern systems of education in Europe derive from schools founded upon religious principles with the primary purpose of training the clergy.

          1.5.4.1.4.1. Pro:
          -> See 1.1.2.1.2.1.1.

        1.5.4.1.5. Pro:
        -> See 1.5.2.2.2.

      1.5.4.2. Pro:
      Western civilization via Christianity has made great contributions to philosophy and modern thought.

        1.5.4.2.1. Pro:
        During the Middle Ages [842], St. Augustine and St. Ambrose both studied earlier philosophers [843] and assimilated Greek and Roman philosophy, the basis of Western thought, into Christian doctrine in order to create a rational interpretation of Christian faith.

        1.5.4.2.2. Pro:
        -> See 1.3.3.4.1.3.3.

        1.5.4.2.3. Con:
        -> See 1.3.6.2.11.2.1.

        1.5.4.2.4. Con:
        -> See 1.3.5.1.5.1.

      1.5.4.3. Pro:
      The current standard calendar [844] used around the globe was adopted for the convenience of the Christian church.

      1.5.4.4. Con:
      The integral role that Christianity has played in Western civilization is not necessarily positive.

        1.5.4.4.1. Pro:
        -> See 1.3.6.3.2.1.

      1.5.4.5. Con:
      The Christian persecution of Pagans [845] in the Roman Empire resulted in the cessation of the Olympics [846] along with other Pagan practices.

      1.5.4.6. Con:
      We have advanced civilizations that hold no ties to Western religion. As such, religion's effect is not obvious.

      1.5.4.7. Pro:
      The major cultural achievements and heritage of many Western countries can be attributed to religion.

        1.5.4.7.1. Pro:
        Religion has been key in the creation and preservation of art and culture.

          1.5.4.7.1.1. Con:
          But, on the other hand, throughout history there have been many cases where ancient temples and works of art have been damaged, or completely destroyed [847], for religious reasons.

          1.5.4.7.1.2. Pro:
          Western Civilization via Christianity has given the world great musical [848] compositions.

            1.5.4.7.1.2.1. Pro:
            "Ode to Joy [849]" by Beethoven and "Hallelujah [850]" by Händel are well-known examples of compositions inspired by religion.

          1.5.4.7.1.3. Con:
          Art also exists independently from religion and from culture.

          1.5.4.7.1.4. Pro:
          Many of the ancient buildings which were built to a high enough standard to survive to the present day were built for religious reasons.

            1.5.4.7.1.4.1. Pro:
            The Hagia Sophia [851], commonly considered to be a World Wonder, was constructed as a result of religion.

              1.5.4.7.1.4.1.1. Con:
              It may be pretty but it's still just a building with a positive function only if religion is considered a good thing. It was built using taxes gathered for Emperor Justinian [852]. The effort put into it could have been put towards infrastructure which would have had positive practical results.

            1.5.4.7.1.4.2. Pro:
            It is theorized that the Stonehenge, commonly considered to be a World Wonder, was constructed as a result of religion.

            1.5.4.7.1.4.3. Con:
            Throughout history religion has had copious amounts of money and power, and consequently the best constructed buildings and monuments were religious. Had the money been elsewhere different long standing buildings may have been built. It cannot be determined which would be subjectively better or worse.

              1.5.4.7.1.4.3.1. Con:
              In a free market economy, the money goes where the consumer's interest is. Followers had to first be interested in spending money on their church in order for them to gain status, power and budget.

                1.5.4.7.1.4.3.1.1. Con:
                The Catholic church sold forgiveness of sins. Not going to hell is pretty high up there when it comes to consumer interest.

                1.5.4.7.1.4.3.1.2. Con:
                The world was not a free market economy at the time when many major religions showed up. Feudalism was not exactly a free market.

                1.5.4.7.1.4.3.1.3. Con:
                Consumer interest in something does not mean it is a good influence on the consumers or the world.

            1.5.4.7.1.4.4. Pro:
            Of the seven wonders [853] of the world, five are absolutely religiously motivated. Without it, we would never had Pyramids, Taj Mahal and the others. The masterpieces of architecture which define us as a species, nonexistent.

              1.5.4.7.1.4.4.1. Con:
              Architecture does not define a species. Humans prosocial [854] is typically what is referenced as making us distinct from other animals as well as language but other animals build things as well. It seems a bit heavy handed to say that a few architectural structures define us as a species.

            1.5.4.7.1.4.5. Con:
            One may argue that religion has created many fascinating architectural wonders and has inspired the arts in general. However, these minor accomplishments can't resurrect the humans and the damage that religion has done unto both people and countries.

              1.5.4.7.1.4.5.1. Pro:
              For example, the islamization [855] of secular Iran [856] and right now of Turkey [857].

            1.5.4.7.1.4.6. Pro:
            These ancient buildings often contribute to the touristic attractiveness of some places, which in turn contributes to the economy.

          1.5.4.7.1.5. Con:
          Heritage preservation by religious institutions is not necessary a good thing. It just means that religions had the control of what to conserve, and what to burn.

            1.5.4.7.1.5.1. Pro:
            Religion was all too often the cause for destruction of art objects [858] and whole cultures.

          1.5.4.7.1.6. Pro:
          Religion has been one of the greatest sources for artistic expression in all of Human history.

            1.5.4.7.1.6.1. Pro:
            Religion had a huge positive impact on the development of architecture, music and the theatre (rites originated from mysteries in ancient Greece [859]).

            1.5.4.7.1.6.2. Con:
            Throughout history religion has had copious amounts of money and power, and consequently many religious works of art were commissioned. Had the money been elsewhere different pieces may have been made. It cannot be determined which would be subjectively better or worse.

            1.5.4.7.1.6.3. Pro:
            Without Christianity, there may never have be the cantata, the concerto, or the symphony. Handel [860], Vivaldi [861], and Bach were Christians who worked to honor God with their work [862].

              1.5.4.7.1.6.3.1. Con:
              All these artists have used religion as an inspiration for their work. That doesn't mean that without religion they wouldn't be inspired by other sources of the human life such as nature etc.

          1.5.4.7.1.7. Pro:
          Institutions like the Roman Catholic Church supported many artists through artistic patronage [863].

        1.5.4.7.2. Con:
        In the same way, many past cultures and much heritage was destroyed because of religion: Americas' conquest [864], Middle East in the current century [865] or many European Pagan cultures [866].

          1.5.4.7.2.1. Pro:
          Native American cultures were destroyed by the introduction of Christianity.

            1.5.4.7.2.1.1. Con:
            Native American cultures were destroyed by diseases and violence that left them a minority.

          1.5.4.7.2.2. Con:
          The Cultural Revolution [867], an explicitly secular movement, led to the destruction [868] of cultural artifacts in China.

        1.5.4.7.3. Con:
        Religion [869] is less important to younger Americans, indicating that it is becoming obsolete.

        1.5.4.7.4. Pro:
        Religious rules are an important source of contemporary laws.

          1.5.4.7.4.1. Con:
          Contemporary law is not based at all on the 10 commandments.

            1.5.4.7.4.1.1. Pro:
            The first four commandments are about God and the sabbath, none of which appears in contemporary western law.

            1.5.4.7.4.1.2. Pro:
            Honoring your parents may be expected but is not required under the law.

            1.5.4.7.4.1.3. Pro:
            Killing is legal under certain circumstances (self defense and wartime).

            1.5.4.7.4.1.4. Pro:
            Coveting goods is not illegal.

            1.5.4.7.4.1.5. Con:
            Theft is illegal.

            1.5.4.7.4.1.6. Con:
            Perjury is illegal.

              1.5.4.7.4.1.6.1. Con:
              Perjury has been watered down to varying degrees [870]. Generally it can only be illegal when lying to the police or under oath.

            1.5.4.7.4.1.7. Pro:
            Adultery is not illegal in most areas [871].

          1.5.4.7.4.2. Pro:
          -> See 1.2.5.2.2.1.1.

          1.5.4.7.4.3. Con:
          Laws based on religious ideals are not always in the best interests of the people (p. 3 [872]).

            1.5.4.7.4.3.1. Con:
            Many unjust laws [873] have a secular origin, so there is no reason why religion should be singled out in this regard.

            1.5.4.7.4.3.2. Con:
            The incorporation of Islamic law with secular Egyptian criminal law has promoted human rights [874].

            1.5.4.7.4.3.3. Pro:
            Religious rules also contributed in some cases to support laws like the death penalty [875].

            1.5.4.7.4.3.4. Pro:
            Legalization of some Drugs [876], legalization of Gay marriage [877], legalization of Abortion [878], are some examples of laws that have traditionally been or are currently contested by religious people.

            1.5.4.7.4.3.5. Pro:
            Some countries still penalize [879] extra-marital sex, homosexuality [880], and same-sex marriage [881].

              1.5.4.7.4.3.5.1. Pro:
              In some Islamic countries, women are incarcerated after being raped [882] since it is considered adultery and thus legally punished.

          1.5.4.7.4.4. Con:
          Mixing religion and legislation forces the religious views of some on the entire population. Since religion is not required to protect the populace it should not be mingling with the law.

          1.5.4.7.4.5. Con:
          The use of the term "secularized" inaccurately implies that religion is responsible for laws and by implication morals that were actually adopted by religion from common sense and our evolutionarily generated impulses [883].

            1.5.4.7.4.5.1. Pro:
            Laws against theft, murder, and other crimes existed long before organized religion.

              1.5.4.7.4.5.1.1. Pro:
              The code of Ur-Nammu, written in the 21st century BCE, was a codified set of laws that included punishments for murder, theft, and other crimes [884].

          1.5.4.7.4.6. Pro:
          In some cases, religious rules have contributed to democracy.

            1.5.4.7.4.6.1. Con:
            Christianity is authoritarian at its core as it has one power above all (God).

              1.5.4.7.4.6.1.1. Pro:
              Christianity and monotheism in general often refer to adherents as sheep in a flock and Christians sometimes characterize themselves as slaves to Christ [885] this is totalitarian.

              1.5.4.7.4.6.1.2. Pro:
              Christianity along with its monotheistic cousins require that one both loves and fears [886] god. This is the basis of a sadomasochistic relationship illustrating the religion's totalitarian nature.

        1.5.4.7.5. Con:
        Religion is only one of the many elements that contribute to culture; culture encompasses religion [887].

    1.5.5. Con:
    Any correlation between the religiosity of societies and their failure or success could have other causes [888].

      1.5.5.1. Pro:
      People tend to turn to religion in a crisis [889], which could skew the results and link failure and religion.

"The United Church and its predecessors managed schools in Ontario and Western Canada – the number ranging from thirteen in 1927 to four in 1966. The United Church also ran a number of day schools in First Nations communities. By 1969, the federal government took over the management or closed all of the United Church residential schools. The residential school system brought harm and cultural dislocation to children by removing them from their families and communities.

Survivors began to recount their experiences of cultural, physical, psychological, and sexual abuse in United Church residential schools and brought legal action against the different bodies responsible for the schools."

Their hair was cut, and they were dressed in the dominant culture fashion, forced to abandon their native languages, forbidden to use their native names, and taught to repudiate their “pagan” traditions. “Let all that is Indian within you die,”was the commission from Rev. A. J. Lippincott during a Carlisle Indian School graduation.

Physical, emotional, and sexual abuses were documented at Christian-run Indian boarding schools. Student punishments included beatings, physical restraints, and isolation in dark cellars. Many students chose to run away."

"One particularly well-known Catholic method of exploitation in the Middle Ages was the practice of selling indulgences, a monetary payment of penalty which, supposedly, absolved one of past sins and/or released one from purgatory after death. It was the selling of indulgences that led the Reformer Martin Luther to post his famous 95 Theses - a document challenging Roman Catholic authority in theological matters, including indulgences and many others. Luther's opposition to the selling of indulgences was not new, however. In most of the Reformation movements stress lay not upon new understandings or doctrines, but on a return to the more authentic and original excellence of tradition."

"Fifty-three miles of shelving. Thirty-five thousand volumes of catalogue. Twelve centuries worth of documents. Housed in one of the most iconic bastions of religion and culture ever, the Vatican’s Secret Archives are the stuff of historical legend—but their existence is absolutely real.

Just the name invokes the mystery and pageantry of the Catholic Church, and prompts the more imaginative to come up with sinister theories about what might lie within. The archives’ indexes are not public—and are only accessible to scholars once they are 75 years old—and they are housed in a fortress-like part of the Vatican.

The secretive nature of the Catholic Church and the potential trove within have fueled years of wild speculation about what was inside. Even today, conspiracy theories abound over its contents—like wacky speculation that the Vatican is hiding extraterrestrial beings inside."

[392] https://greatergood.berkeley.edu/article/item/morality_animals
"In The Bonobo and the Atheist, renowned primatologist Frans de Waal argues that moral behavior in humans is not predicated on religion. Drawing from extensive research on animals—primarily bonobos and chimpanzees, our nearest primate relatives—as well as research on fossil records of early hominids, he shows how evidence of moral sentiments, like empathy and altruism, predate the advent of religion by millennia and co-evolved in non-human primates as well as in humans.
[...]
Since altruism, empathy, and gratitude all underpin moral behavior, finding them in our fellow mammals suggests that they run deep in our brain biology and did not come about because of moral reasoning or religion. In fact, probably the opposite is true—religion developed because of our innate capacities for caring."
[393] https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_and_evil#Transcendental_realism
[394] https://plato.stanford.edu/entries/moral-arguments-god/#HisMorArgForGodExi
"Kant held that a rational, moral being must necessarily will “the highest good,” which consists of a world in which people are both morally good and happy, and in which moral virtue is the condition for happiness. The latter condition implies that this end must be sought solely by moral action. However, Kant held that a person cannot rationally will such an end without believing that moral actions can successfully achieve such an end, and this requires a belief that the causal structure of nature is conducive to the achievement of this end by moral means. This is equivalent to belief in God, a moral being who is ultimately responsible for the character of the natural world."

"The issue of the prophetic narrations that seem to contradict one another or contrary to Quranic texts or even other facts have been debated and discussed by the hadith scholars long time ago. called ‘contradictions’ which in reality can cause a significant impact on the accountability of hadiths as one of the main sources for the Muslims civilization. Hence, this paper aims to analyse and review the method of

"Through proverbs and stories, the Bible reveals much about what it means to parent in a godly way. Much is demonstrated through narrative examples of parents raising their children in good and . . . well, not-so-good ways.

Some of those bad examples lead people to think that anything the Bible has to say about parenting should be discredited. But I believe the illustrations of bad parenting in the Bible are meant to show us what not to do—as most bad examples are intended to do.
[...]

"How is it that religions - which supposedly espouse peace, love and harmony - are so commonly connected with intolerance and violent aggression? Social scientists are divided on the issue. Others such as Richard Dawkins believe that because religions fuel certainties and sanctify martyrdom, they are often a root cause of conflict. Meanwhile, Timothy Sisk claims that both hierarchical religious traditions (such as Shi´ism) and non-hierarchical traditions (such as Buddhism) can both be vulnerable to interpretation of canon to justify or even provide warrants for violent action.
Religious violence has been rising for years"

"All able-bodied Muslims are expected to fast during the month of Ramadan from dawn until dusk. This is complete abstinence from food, drink and sexual relations.
[...]
Dehydration – Dehydration is a common occurrence during a fast. The body continues to lose water and salts through breathing, sweat and urine; the quantity of water loss will vary depending on the weather, how much you had to drink before your fast, the degree of physical exertion and the ability of the kidneys to retain water and salts. 

Stress- Lack of food and water, changes of routine and shorter periods of sleep can all collude to increase stress levels. Hence it is important to address any potential sources of stress in order to minimise harmful effects. 

Obesity- For the unwary, or those lacking in caution, food consumed during the pre-dawn and dusk meals may lead to some unintended weight gain."

"Most religious belief systems maintain that some natural events are caused by divine intervention. Exploration of the manner in which believers attribute events to supernatural causes is thus one fruitful way of studying the psychology of religion. The attributional processes of evangelical Christian college students were explored by means of a questionnaire containing several brief descriptions of incidents, each followed by rating scales on which subjects attributed causality for that event. Individual differences in the tendency to invoke supernatural explanations were found, and the tendency to invoke such explanations was positively correlated with other measures of religious belief and practice."

[666] https://cstarendal.medium.com/the-dunbar-number-40c826da34c3
"The British professor in anthropology Robin Dunbar figured that the same principle should apply to all primates (including humans). And published in 1992 an article called “Neocortex size as a constraint on group size in primates” in the Journal of Human evolution. This article contained a model called “the social brain hypothesis” that predicted social factors about primates based on the size of their neocortex.

“The Tamarin monkey has a neocortex/brain size ratio of about 2.3 and an average social group of size of about 5 members. On the other hand, a Macaque monkey has a neocortex/brain size ratio of around 3.8 but a very large average group size of about 40 members.”

Dunbar then used data for 38 different primates to predict a human “mean group size” of 148 (casually rounded to 150) and an “intimate circle size” of 12-15."

"Religion played a major role in some of the largest book burning events after the Roman Empire began to decline around 500 AD.
[...]
The Catholic Church organized numerous book burnings throughout the medieval period. In the 13th century, Pope Gregory IX ordered the burning of the Jewish theological work The Talmud along with “those books in which you find errors of this sort you shall cause to be burned at the stake,” according to the Jewish Virtual Library.

As a consequence of the order, 24 wagonloads containing thousands of volumes were burned in France in 1242.

Works of religion were frequent targets by the Catholic Church. Holy Roman Emperor Charles V joined others in burning and censoring works from the theologian Martin Luther, whose teachings paved the way for Protestantism."
[792] https://www.history.com/this-day-in-history/galileo-is-accused-of-heresy
"Galileo was ordered to turn himself in to the Holy Office to begin trial for holding the belief that the Earth revolves around the sun, which was deemed heretical by the Catholic Church. 
[...]
This time, Galileo’s technical argument didn’t win the day. On June 22, 1633, the Church handed down the following order: “We pronounce, judge, and declare, that you, the said Galileo… have rendered yourself vehemently suspected by this Holy Office of heresy, that is, of having believed and held the doctrine (which is false and contrary to the Holy and Divine Scriptures) that the sun is the center of the world, and that it does not move from east to west, and that the earth does move, and is not the center of the world.”
[...]
Galileo agreed not to teach the heresy anymore and spent the rest of his life under house arrest."

"Second, in the years since the First Council of Nicaea in AD 325,[b] the excess leap days introduced by the Julian algorithm had caused the calendar to drift such that the (Northern) spring equinox was occurring well before its nominal 21 March date. This date was important to the Christian churches because it is fundamental to the calculation of the date of Easter. To reinstate the association, the reform advanced the date by 10 days: Thursday 4 October 1582 was followed by Friday 15 October 1582.[3] In addition, the reform also altered the lunar cycle used by the Church to calculate the date for Easter, because astronomical new moons were occurring four days before the calculated dates."

"The Code of Ur-NammuOffsite Link is the oldest surviving law code. It was written in the Sumerian language c. 2100-2050 BCE.
[...]
The laws are arranged in casuistic form of if-(crime), then-(punishment) — a pattern to be followed in nearly all subsequent codes. For the oldest extant law-code known to history, it is considered remarkably advanced, because it institutes fines of monetary compensation for bodily damage, as opposed to the later lex talionisOffsite Link (‘eye for an eye’) principle of Babylonian law; however, the capital crimes of murder, robbery, adultery and rape are punished with death."
